Multiclass Boundary Refinement with Neural Cellular Automata for Remote Sensing Land Cover Segmentation

Semantic segmentation of remote sensing imagery often places class boundaries a few pixels away from where they truly lie, and this position error is concentrated in the thin band measured by Boundary IoU (BIoU). We treat boundary refinement as the iterated application of a small learned local rule: the Boundary Refinement Neural Cellular Automaton (BRNCA), that starts from the class probabilities of a frozen, pre-trained segmenter and corrects them cell by cell. We generalise the binary rNCA to the multiclass case by keeping the visible channels on the probability simplex through an Aitchison (log-space) update and by adding free hidden channels. The best configuration improves the mean BIoU over the base segmenter by 2.36 points (from 46.63 to 48.99). The gain is concentrated on the irregular woodland class (+8.5 points BIoU); this suggests that position error is correlated with the fractal dimension of the class boundaries.

Refining Multiclass Land-Cover Boundaries in Remote-Sensing Images with Neural Cellular Automata

Class boundaries in remote-sensing segmentation can be displaced by just a few pixels, concentrating errors in the narrow region evaluated by Boundary IoU. BRNCA starts from a frozen segmenter’s class probabilities and repeatedly applies a learned local correction. It extends binary neural cellular automata to multiple classes using free hidden channels and an Aitchison update in log space that keeps visible channels on the probability simplex. The best model raised mean Boundary IoU by 2.36 points, from 46.63 to 48.99. Woodland, with its irregular boundary, gained 8.5 points. The authors suggest a link between localization error and the fractal dimension of class boundaries.

2412.20993v2 · Efficiently Scaling LLM Reasoning with Certaindex
compare claims and qualifiers · word sequence similarity 0.336

Efficiently Scaling LLM Reasoning with Certaindex

Test-time reasoning algorithms such as chain-of-thought, self-consistency, and MCTS enhance LLM problem-solving but can wastefully generate many tokens without improving accuracy. At the same time, we observe that these algorithms exhibit answer stabilization: their intermediate solutions often cease to change after a certain point, and further investment of compute does not change their final answer. To quantify this phenomenon, we introduce Certaindex, an algorithm-agnostic metric measuring this evolving stability, signaling when further computation is unlikely to alter the final result. Certaindex is lightweight, can accelerate reasoning program inference via early exit, and further enables dynamic token allocation, gang scheduling, and many opportunities when integrated with real-world LLM serving systems. To quantify real-world benefits, we built Certaindex as a scheduler into Dynasor, our reasoning-aware LLM serving system, and demonstrate up to 50% compute savings and 3.3x higher throughput in real workloads with no accuracy drop. Our code is available at https://github.com/hao-ai-lab/Dynasor.git

Scaling Reasoning Efficiently by Tracking Answer Stability with Certaindex

Chain-of-thought, self-consistency and MCTS can improve reasoning while consuming tokens after the answer has already stabilized. Certaindex tracks stability across intermediate solutions, providing a lightweight signal across reasoning algorithms that more computation is unlikely to alter an answer. A serving system can use it to stop early, assign token budgets dynamically or schedule work in groups. The authors implemented this scheduler in Dynasor and report up to 50% less computation and 3.3× higher throughput on real workloads with no measured loss in accuracy. Code is available at https://github.com/hao-ai-lab/Dynasor.git

MultiRisk: Multiple Risk Control via Iterative Score Thresholding

As generative AI systems are increasingly deployed in real-world applications, regulating multiple dimensions of model behavior has become essential. We focus on test-time filtering: a lightweight mechanism for behavior control that compares performance scores to estimated thresholds, and modifies outputs when these bounds are violated. We formalize the problem of enforcing multiple risk constraints with user-defined priorities, and introduce two efficient dynamic programming algorithms that leverage this sequential structure. The first, MULTIRISK-BASE, provides a direct finite-sample procedure for selecting thresholds, while the second, MULTIRISK, leverages data exchangeability to guarantee simultaneous control of the risks. Under mild assumptions, we show that MULTIRISK achieves nearly tight control of all constraint risks. The analysis requires an intricate iterative argument, upper bounding the risks by introducing several forms of intermediate symmetrized risk functions, and carefully lower bounding the risks by recursively counting jumps in symmetrized risk functions between appropriate risk levels. We evaluate our framework on a three-constraint Large Language Model alignment task using the PKU-SafeRLHF dataset, where the goal is to maximize helpfulness subject to multiple safety constraints, and where scores are generated by a Large Language Model judge and a perplexity filter. Our experimental results show that our algorithm can control each individual risk at close to the target level.

Iteratively Choosing Score Thresholds to Control Multiple Risks in MultiRisk

The authors study a test-time filter that modifies model outputs when their scores cross estimated thresholds. Its objective is to control multiple risks with user-defined priorities. Two dynamic programs exploit the ordering of the constraints. MULTIRISK-BASE selects thresholds directly from a finite sample, while MULTIRISK uses exchangeability to guarantee simultaneous control. Under mild assumptions its risk bounds are nearly tight. The proof bounds risks iteratively through symmetrized risk functions and counts changes between risk levels to establish lower bounds. On a three-constraint PKU-SafeRLHF task, the method sought to maximize helpfulness subject to safety requirements, using scores from an LLM judge and a perplexity filter. Individual risks were controlled close to their targets.

Development of a Thermodynamics of Human Cognition and Human Culture

Inspired by foundational studies in classical and quantum physics, and by information retrieval studies in quantum information theory, we prove that the notions of 'energy' and 'entropy' can be consistently introduced in human language and, more generally, in human culture. More explicitly, if energy is attributed to words according to their frequency of appearance in a text, then the ensuing energy levels are distributed non-classically, namely, they obey Bose-Einstein, rather than Maxwell-Boltzmann, statistics, as a consequence of the genuinely 'quantum indistinguishability' of the words that appear in the text. Secondly, the 'quantum entanglement' due to the way meaning is carried by a text reduces the (von Neumann) entropy of the words that appear in the text, a behaviour which cannot be explained within classical (thermodynamic or information) entropy. We claim here that this 'quantum-type behaviour is valid in general in human language', namely, any text is conceptually more concrete than the words composing it, which entails that the entropy of the overall text decreases. In addition, we provide examples taken from cognition, where quantization of energy appears in categorical perception, and from culture, where entities collaborate, thus 'entangle', to decrease overall entropy. We use these findings to propose the development of a new 'non-classical thermodynamic theory' for human cognition, which also covers broad parts of human culture and its artefacts and bridges concepts with quantum physics entities.

Toward a Thermodynamic Account of Human Culture and Cognition

Drawing on classical and quantum physics and on quantum approaches to information retrieval, the authors develop definitions of energy and entropy for language and culture. Assigning energy to words from their frequency in a text gives energy levels following Bose–Einstein statistics. They attribute this departure from Maxwell–Boltzmann statistics to word indistinguishability. They also argue that meaning entangles words and lowers their von Neumann entropy in a way classical accounts cannot explain. Their broader hypothesis is that a text conveys a more concrete concept than its constituent words, reducing the entropy of the whole. Examples include energy quantization in categorical perception and cultural entities whose collaboration reduces entropy. These observations motivate a nonclassical thermodynamics of cognition, culture and artefacts that connects concepts with quantum entities.

GPT Takes the Bar Exam

Nearly all jurisdictions in the United States require a professional license exam, commonly referred to as "the Bar Exam," as a precondition for law practice. To even sit for the exam, most jurisdictions require that an applicant completes at least seven years of post-secondary education, including three years at an accredited law school. In addition, most test-takers also undergo weeks to months of further, exam-specific preparation. Despite this significant investment of time and capital, approximately one in five test-takers still score under the rate required to pass the exam on their first try. In the face of a complex task that requires such depth of knowledge, what, then, should we expect of the state of the art in "AI?" In this research, we document our experimental evaluation of the performance of OpenAI's `text-davinci-003` model, often-referred to as GPT-3.5, on the multistate multiple choice (MBE) section of the exam. While we find no benefit in fine-tuning over GPT-3.5's zero-shot performance at the scale of our training data, we do find that hyperparameter optimization and prompt engineering positively impacted GPT-3.5's zero-shot performance. For best prompt and parameters, GPT-3.5 achieves a headline correct rate of 50.3% on a complete NCBE MBE practice exam, significantly in excess of the 25% baseline guessing rate, and performs at a passing rate for both Evidence and Torts. GPT-3.5's ranking of responses is also highly-correlated with correctness; its top two and top three choices are correct 71% and 88% of the time, respectively, indicating very strong non-entailment performance. While our ability to interpret these results is limited by nascent scientific understanding of LLMs and the proprietary nature of GPT, we believe that these results strongly suggest that an LLM will pass the MBE component of the Bar Exam in the near future.

How Does GPT Perform on the Multistate Bar Examination?

The authors frame the US bar exam as a demanding test of AI. Nearly all jurisdictions require a licensing exam, and most require at least seven years of education after secondary school, including three years at an accredited law school, to sit it. Candidates commonly spend weeks or months preparing, yet about a fifth fall short on the first attempt. The study evaluated text-davinci-003 (GPT-3.5) on the multiple-choice MBE. Fine-tuning on the available data did not improve on zero-shot performance. Adjusting prompts and hyperparameters did. With the best settings the model answered 50.3% correctly on a full NCBE practice MBE, above the 25% guessing baseline, and reached passing rates in Evidence and Torts. Its top two choices included the answer 71% of the time and its top three 88%, indicating an ability to rule out incorrect alternatives. The authors cite limited understanding of LLMs and restricted access to the proprietary model, and predict that an LLM will soon pass the MBE.

ToM2Vec: Continuous Theory-of-Mind Tokens for Multi-Agent Coordination

Forming an accurate theory-of-mind (ToM) is key to successful multi-agent collaboration and requires reasoning under uncertainty to capture multiple possible partner states. This is hard to do in the text representation space native to large language model (LLM) agents, which more easily describe a single partner state. To address this challenge, we introduce ToM2Vec, which learns to form abstract theories-of-mind in a rich latent space via a fixed budget of continuous reasoning tokens. During training, supervision for these tokens comes from the partner's actual thoughts, which the ToM tokens are trained to abstract and match. At test-time, ToM tokens are generated by each agent and act as conditioners for that agent's thoughts and actions. Across several environments covering collaborative, competitive, and mixed-motive settings (Overcooked-AI, multiparty negotiation, Decrypto, Hanabi, and Collab Escape/Capture), we evaluate ToM2Vec's ability to support multi-agent interactions and collaboration, finding consistent improvements over the base model and a variety of competitive trained and prompted baselines. We show that latent reasoning is critical, with performance dropping when reasoning is expressed in text. Moreover, we show that ToM2Vec training generalizes to partner model families not seen at train-time, including a proprietary model (GPT-5.4). In our analysis, we provide evidence that the latent tokens encode a ToM, rather than task-specific information: ToM2Vec's tokens improve the agent's ability to directly answer questions about partner beliefs, desires, and next actions.

Coordinating Agents with Continuous Representations of Partner Beliefs in ToM2Vec

A collaborating agent may need to reason about several possible partner states at once. Text-based reasoning tends to favor a single description. ToM2Vec instead learns a fixed number of continuous tokens that represent uncertainty about the partner. Training uses the partner’s actual thoughts as supervision. At inference, each agent produces its own tokens and conditions its reasoning and actions on them. Across Overcooked-AI, multiparty negotiation, Decrypto, Hanabi and Collab Escape/Capture, the method improved on the base model and trained or prompted alternatives. Expressing the reasoning in text reduced performance. The learned representation transferred to unseen partner families including GPT-5.4. Better answers about partners’ beliefs, desires and next actions provide evidence that the tokens capture a theory of mind extending beyond task-specific information.

Preference Is Not Permission: Personalizing the Auto-Purchase Line in Agentic Commerce

Personalized shopping benchmarks ask which item fits the user; we ask how far an agent may commit on that item, and pose learning the individual's auto-purchase line as a task. The boundary is evidence-conditioned and three-valued: whether committing is appropriate depends on the sufficiency of product information (warrant), reversibility, price against the user's norm, and urgency, not only on fit; and the natural output is don't buy, confirm first, or buy automatically. Across sixteen instruction-tuned models, autonomous commitment is misaligned with a human authorization reference in which fifteen annotators, role-playing the delegating principal from a given profile, choose the furthest action they would allow without confirmation: models commit even when fit is low and when their own evidence flag reports the warrant missing. This is overreach. The line is individual: these raters disagree, yet the disagreement is structured and partly predictable from a person's own other labels. A shallow learner on a few dozen of a person's own labels predicts their held-out auto-purchase line at a median of 0.79 by 30-40 labels, outperforming every evaluated prompt$\times$model configuration, including a per-annotator oracle over 80 configurations; the learned boundary does not transfer across domains. We release the counterfactual benchmark, human authorization reference, and annotation protocol after acceptance.

Learning When a Shopping Agent Has Permission to Buy Automatically

Shopping benchmarks usually evaluate product fit. This study asks which action a particular shopper would permit: decline, request confirmation or buy automatically. Relevant factors include the evidence about the product, ability to undo the purchase, personal price norms and urgency. Fifteen annotators took the roles of users with supplied profiles and indicated the furthest action they would authorize. Sixteen instruction-tuned models sometimes committed despite poor fit or their own indication of missing evidence. Annotators differed systematically: with 30–40 labels per person, a shallow model predicted held-out authorization boundaries with a reported median of 0.79. It surpassed all tested prompt/model combinations, including an oracle choosing among 80 configurations for each annotator. The learned boundary did not transfer across domains. The authors plan to release the counterfactual benchmark, authorization labels and annotation protocol after acceptance.

Towards Fully Automated Manga Translation

We tackle the problem of machine translation of manga, Japanese comics. Manga translation involves two important problems in machine translation: context-aware and multimodal translation. Since text and images are mixed up in an unstructured fashion in Manga, obtaining context from the image is essential for manga translation. However, it is still an open problem how to extract context from image and integrate into MT models. In addition, corpus and benchmarks to train and evaluate such model is currently unavailable. In this paper, we make the following four contributions that establishes the foundation of manga translation research. First, we propose multimodal context-aware translation framework. We are the first to incorporate context information obtained from manga image. It enables us to translate texts in speech bubbles that cannot be translated without using context information (e.g., texts in other speech bubbles, gender of speakers, etc.). Second, for training the model, we propose the approach to automatic corpus construction from pairs of original manga and their translations, by which large parallel corpus can be constructed without any manual labeling. Third, we created a new benchmark to evaluate manga translation. Finally, on top of our proposed methods, we devised a first comprehensive system for fully automated manga translation.

Working Toward End-to-End Automation of Manga Translation

Machine translation of Japanese comics must address both context dependence and the combination of text with visual information. Because manga intermixes text and images in an unstructured layout, image-derived context is important for translation, yet extracting and integrating that context into translation models remains an open problem. Suitable training corpora and evaluation benchmarks were also unavailable. This work makes four contributions: a multimodal, context-aware translation framework that incorporates information from manga images; an automatic method for constructing parallel training corpora from original manga and their translations without manual labeling; a benchmark for evaluating manga translation; and a comprehensive system for fully automated manga translation. The framework enables translation of speech-bubble text that cannot be translated adequately without contextual information, such as text in other speech bubbles or information about the speaker's gender. The authors present the framework as the first to incorporate context obtained from manga images and the system as the first comprehensive system for fully automated manga translation.

Deep versus Broad Technology Search and the Timing of Innovation Impact

This study offers a new perspective on the depth-versus-breadth debate in innovation strategy, by modeling inventive search within dynamic collective knowledge systems, and underscoring the importance of timing for technological impact. Using frontier machine learning to project patent citation networks in hyperbolic space, we analyze 4.9 million U.S. patents to examine how search strategies give rise to distinct temporal patterns in impact accumulation. We find that inventions based on deep search, which relies on a specialized understanding of complex recombination structures, drive higher short-term impact through early adoption within specialized communities, but face diminishing returns as innovations become "locked-in" with limited diffusion potential. Conversely, when inventions are grounded in broad search that spans disparate domains, they encounter initial resistance but achieve wider diffusion and greater long-term impact by reaching cognitively diverse audiences. Individual inventions require both depth and breadth for stable impact. Organizations can strategically balance approaches across multiple inventions: using depth to build reliable technological infrastructure while pursuing breadth to expand applications. We advance innovation theory by demonstrating how deep and broad search strategies distinctly shape the timing and trajectory of technological impact, and how individual inventors and organizations can leverage these mechanisms to balance exploitation and exploration.

When Deep and Broad Technology Searches Have Their Impact on Innovation

How quickly do inventions rooted in deep or broad searches have an impact? The authors embedded citation networks for 4.9 million US patents in hyperbolic space to analyze patterns of adoption. Deep searches that recombine specialized knowledge gained early uptake within expert communities, then faced diminishing returns as their reach narrowed. Searches across disparate fields encountered initial resistance but eventually reached more diverse audiences and had greater long-run impact. Individual inventions benefited from both depth and breadth for sustained impact. At the organizational level, the authors suggest using deep search to develop reliable infrastructure and broad search across a portfolio to expand its applications.

Large Empirical Case Study: Go-Explore adapted for AI Red Team Testing

Production LLM agents with tool-using capabilities require security testing despite their safety training. We adapt Go-Explore to evaluate GPT-4o-mini across 28 experimental runs spanning six research questions. We find that random-seed variance dominates algorithmic parameters, yielding an 8x spread in outcomes; single-seed comparisons are unreliable, while multi-seed averaging materially reduces variance in our setup. Reward shaping consistently harms performance, causing exploration collapse in 94% of runs or producing 18 false positives with zero verified attacks. In our environment, simple state signatures outperform complex ones. For comprehensive security testing, ensembles provide attack-type diversity, whereas single agents optimize coverage within a given attack type. Overall, these results suggest that seed variance and targeted domain knowledge can outweigh algorithmic sophistication when testing safety-trained models.

Evaluating Go-Explore for AI Red Team Testing in a Large Empirical Study

An empirical evaluation of Go-Explore with GPT-4o-mini studied six questions in 28 runs. Outcomes differed eightfold between random seeds, a larger effect than the algorithmic settings examined, and averaging across seeds reduced variation. Under the tested reward-shaping schemes, exploration collapsed in 94% of runs or yielded 18 false positives and no verified attacks. Simpler state signatures performed better in this environment. Ensembles covered more types of failure, while individual agents provided greater coverage within particular types. The findings highlight the importance of seeds and domain knowledge in evaluating agents that use tools.

2012.15484v2 · Seeing is Knowing! Fact-based Visual Question Answering using Knowledge Graph Embeddings
compare claims and qualifiers · word sequence similarity 0.1982

Seeing is Knowing! Fact-based Visual Question Answering using Knowledge Graph Embeddings

Fact-based Visual Question Answering (FVQA), a challenging variant of VQA, requires a QA-system to include facts from a diverse knowledge graph (KG) in its reasoning process to produce an answer. Large KGs, especially common-sense KGs, are known to be incomplete, i.e., not all non-existent facts are always incorrect. Therefore, being able to reason over incomplete KGs for QA is a critical requirement in real-world applications that has not been addressed extensively in the literature. We develop a novel QA architecture that allows us to reason over incomplete KGs, something current FVQA state-of-the-art (SOTA) approaches lack due to their critical reliance on fact retrieval. We use KG Embeddings, a technique widely used for KG completion, for the downstream task of FVQA. We also employ a new image representation technique we call 'Image-as-Knowledge' to enable this capability, alongside a simple one-step CoAttention mechanism to attend to text and image during QA. Our FVQA architecture is faster during inference time, being O(m), as opposed to existing FVQA SOTA methods which are O(N log N), where m = number of vertices, N = number of edges = O(m^2). KG embeddings are shown to hold complementary information to word embeddings: a combination of both metrics permits performance comparable to SOTA methods in the standard answer retrieval task, and significantly better (26% absolute) in the proposed missing-edge reasoning task.

Knowledge Graph Embeddings for Answering Fact-Based Visual Questions

Fact-based visual question answering must combine image evidence with information in a knowledge graph. Commonsense graphs are incomplete, so an absent edge need not represent a false statement. Methods that depend on retrieving the right fact struggle under this condition. The authors use embeddings developed for graph completion, an Image-as-Knowledge representation, and a single co-attention step over text and images. They report O(m) inference for m vertices, compared with O(N log N) for existing methods, where N counts edges and is O(m²). Combining knowledge-graph and word embeddings matched leading methods on ordinary answer retrieval and improved performance by 26% absolute on the authors’ missing-edge task.

2112.11769v1 · On Theoretical Complexity and Boolean Satisfiability
compare claims and qualifiers · word sequence similarity 0.1974

On Theoretical Complexity and Boolean Satisfiability

Theoretical complexity is a vital subfield of computer science that enables us to mathematically investigate computation and answer many interesting queries about the nature of computational problems. It provides theoretical tools to assess time and space requirements of computations along with assessing the difficultly of problems - classifying them accordingly. It also garners at its core one of the most important problems in mathematics, namely, the $\textbf{P vs. NP}$ millennium problem. In essence, this problem asks whether solution and verification reside on two different levels of difficulty. In this thesis, we introduce some of the most central concepts in the Theory of Computing, giving an overview of how computation can be abstracted using Turing machines. Further, we introduce the two most famous problem complexity classes $\textbf{P}$ and $\textbf{NP}$ along with the relationship between them. In addition, we explicate the concept of problem reduction and how it is an essential tool for making hardness comparisons between different problems. Later, we present the problem of Boolean Satisfiability (SAT) which lies at the center of NP-complete problems. We then explore some of its tractable as well as intractable variants such as Horn-SAT and 3-SAT, respectively. Last but not least, we establish polynomial-time reductions from 3-SAT to some of the famous NP-complete graph problems, namely, Clique Finding, Hamiltonian Cycle Finding, and 3-Coloring.

Boolean Satisfiability as a Lens on Computational Complexity

Computational complexity classifies problems by the time and memory needed to solve them. A central question, P versus NP, concerns the relationship between finding a solution and verifying one. This thesis uses Turing machines to introduce a mathematical model of computation, defines P and NP and explains how reductions compare problem difficulty. Boolean satisfiability illustrates the distinction between tractable and intractable variants through Horn-SAT and 3-SAT. The final constructions reduce 3-SAT in polynomial time to clique finding, Hamiltonian cycle finding and graph 3-coloring.

2610.29281v1 · Flat-RLVR: Sharpness-Aware Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards for Reasoning Capacity in LLMs
compare claims and qualifiers · word sequence similarity 0.1919

Flat-RLVR: Sharpness-Aware Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards for Reasoning Capacity in LLMs

Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR), particularly through group-relative policy optimization (GRPO), has emerged as a central paradigm for the post-training of large language models (LLMs). While RLVR is highly effective at enhancing the reasoning capabilities of LLMs, it commonly induces \textit{distribution sharpening}, whereby the policy distribution becomes excessively peaked and rare-yet-correct trajectories are progressively suppressed, thus diminishing sample diversity. To this end, we promote flat-reward optima in RLVR to smooth the policy distribution. We theoretically link flat reward RLVR to reduced distribution sharpening, and empirically show improved sample diversity while maintaining single-attempt accuracy. Our method, called \texttt{Flat}-RLVR, searches for flat reward regions via sharpness-aware minimization during LLM post-training, combining this with GRPO variants to achieve better trade-offs between sampling efficiency and reasoning capacity.

Sharpness-Aware RL with Verifiable Rewards for Language-Model Reasoning in Flat-RLVR

Verifiable-reward training, including GRPO, improves reasoning but can concentrate a language model’s probability on a narrow set of outputs and suppress rare correct solutions. Flat-RLVR uses sharpness-aware minimization to seek flat reward regions during post-training and can be combined with GRPO variants. The authors connect flatter optima to reduced concentration analytically. Their experiments report more diverse samples while preserving single-attempt accuracy, improving the trade-off between sampling efficiency and reasoning performance.

2512.24901v1 · Spectral Graph Neural Networks for Cognitive Task Classification in fMRI Connectomes
compare claims and qualifiers · word sequence similarity 0.134

Spectral Graph Neural Networks for Cognitive Task Classification in fMRI Connectomes

Cognitive task classification using machine learning plays a central role in decoding brain states from neuroimaging data. By integrating machine learning with brain network analysis, complex connectivity patterns can be extracted from functional magnetic resonance imaging connectomes. This process transforms raw blood-oxygen-level-dependent (BOLD) signals into interpretable representations of cognitive processes. Graph neural networks (GNNs) further advance this paradigm by modeling brain regions as nodes and functional connections as edges, capturing topological dependencies and multi-scale interactions that are often missed by conventional approaches. Our proposed SpectralBrainGNN model, a spectral convolution framework based on graph Fourier transforms (GFT) computed via normalized Laplacian eigendecomposition. Experiments on the Human Connectome Project-Task (HCPTask) dataset demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach, achieving a classification accuracy of 96.25\%. The implementation is publicly available at https://github.com/gnnplayground/SpectralBrainGNN to support reproducibility and future research.

Classifying Cognitive Tasks from fMRI Connectomes with Spectral Graph Networks

Functional MRI connectomes represent brain regions and their connections as a graph. A graph model can capture multiscale and topological patterns in blood-oxygen-level-dependent signals that conventional cognitive-task classifiers may miss. SpectralBrainGNN uses spectral convolution with a graph Fourier transform derived from the normalized graph Laplacian. It achieved 96.25% task classification accuracy on the Human Connectome Project task dataset. The authors provide code at https://github.com/gnnplayground/SpectralBrainGNN.
